These projects show great success. As the Federal Government, we are planning a federal vaccination campaign to be supported by numerous accompanying measures. Question 4: The Federal Government has agreed with the federal states that the Covid vaccinations will be carried out jointly. The Federal Government takes over the procurement and supply of the vaccines, the entire financing and also sets out the framework conditions, such as the prioritisation to be observed. The practical implementation is with the federal states in cooperation with the established area. This is important because, not least because of regional circumstances, different approaches are necessary. The Federal Government takes its supervisory responsibility very seriously in this regard. In addition to the current political vote, many voting sessions are taking place, on which I have already commented on Question No 2. On question 6: In order to counter the flood of misinformation with the best possible information, my ministry is constantly putting facts about Fakenews on the website as well as on the Ministry's social media channels. In addition, target groups have been identified on the basis of the corresponding surveys. The communication strategy is constantly being adapted, and relevant information materials are being developed. These are available online in different languages, and they have also been provided to various stakeholders, such as religious communities. Exchange with these is important in order to raise additional information needs. Relevant to further increase the vaccination rate – and at this point I would like to expressly thank the competent federal states for their excellent work – are as low-end vaccines as possible. In addition, we are constantly monitoring measures to increase the rate of vaccination in other countries, such as incentive models, and of course we are also looking at exactly how the vaccination rate in Burgenland will develop with the vaccination. Question 7: All ministries are committed to increasing the rate of vaccination in their areas. Implementation steps are discussed in regular bilateral discussions and meetings, on the other hand also in larger rounds such as the Corona Commission or the Coronataskforce. There is also a weekly vote on the government campaign “Austria immunises” with various ministries. Belakovich.]] In addition, on an organisational level, weekly meetings with the national vaccination coordinators and meetings with the national health experts and the national anity directors take place every two weeks. Question 14: Following Covid-19 vaccinations, allergic reactions are more frequently observed than after conventional vaccinations. Question 15: Apart from the fact that we fortunately have enough vaccine in the country, each person in Austria can book a vaccination date on time and at the place of residence. Therefore, an extension of the circle of health professionals is not currently under planning. On the question 16: The Corona Commission does not recommend the implementation of a population-wide but a representative seroprevalence study. The BMBWF has already conducted three prevalence studies in cooperation with various organisations. The implementation of a large-scale representative antibody study is foreseen in a timely manner. The detailed planning is currently being carried out between my department and the science department. An antibody test does not give a reliable statement about individual protection. Therefore, a wide-ranging performance of such tests does not seem useful, since this would lead to a supposed safety for the individual. That is, we are making an antibody test throughout Austria, which is representative to be able to look in and assess the epidemiological situation. On a personal level, this is not recommended except in the case of specific questions, because of which it is recommended by the doctor who is a doctor. On the question 18: The implementation of a large-scale representative antibody study is planned in a timely manner and is currently being prepared between the scientific department and my department. This is done in good coordination with scientific and strategic experts. Based on EU specifications, evidence of neutralising antibodies cannot be used for the green pass. Population-wide antibody testing is currently not planned. The extension of a prevalence study to children of non-vaccinated age is currently under examination. On question 19: Of course, the Austrian initiative, which is located in the BKA, continues to run. It has been adapted to the new conditions and challenges of this phase of the vaccination campaign over the summer. In addition to the Austrian vaccination campaign, my department focuses on specific stakeholders communication and knowledge transfer. Question 20: I have always said that we will make a decision by the end of September. Discussions and evaluations have not yet been completed. Thank you.

Unfortunately, there are also countries which are very poor, these are Vienna, Carinthia and as a final light the Burgenland, which are unfortunately very sparse and negligent as regards the registration. The clear call for this follow-up report is therefore: We should register more data and thus increase transparency. Finally, it is clear that the ministry here has carried out the homework to a great extent. The portal was expanded and made more user-friendly. ParlaMint-AT_2021-09-22-027-XXVII-NRSITZ-00121_d7e31825	Next speaker: Mr David Stögmüller. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2021-09-22-027-XXVII-NRSITZ-00121_d7e31849	Madam President. Madam President-in-Office of the Council! Ladies and gentlemen! This morning we discussed the findings of the investigative committee on corruption and alleged law-purchasing and the importance and importance of transparency. Now we are talking about the transparency database and the criticism of the Court of Auditors. This is part of a convolution of the Court of Auditors which we so to speak reject today. The transparency database is neither a database nor a truly transparent one if one can say so. I would now like to explain to you more precisely why this is so and what could be improved. Let us, however, perhaps begin in general once more! The transparency database does not have the objective and purpose of creating transparency in the question of who has received how much support. It is intended to serve as a basis for transparent presentation of the overall range of available funding, but not to inform the general public of the concrete persons, companies and how much they have received in support. That's not her job. The introduction of data is intended to give funding bodies an overview of whether a particular person, a particular project et cetera, has already been supported in this sense, in order to avoid multiple funding on the one hand, and on the other hand to simplify as regards necessary evidence and in the control of application requirements. The aim is to ensure that funding is not limited to a few, but that several different organisations can benefit from it. However, if these data are not recorded there, of course no transparency will be created. That's exactly the point: In order to operate a functioning database efficiently, it takes exactly this data, and to do so, the data from this database must also be properly queryed. The Court of Auditors criticises the design of the rights of access to the transparency database as inappropriate, restrictive and complicated. A database that does not allow down-to-date queries does not bring transparency or fulfil the purpose of a database. The objectives of the transparency database – transparency, prevention of abuse and control – were not actually achieved six years after its introduction and after the BMF had spent a total of EUR 13.6 million, the Court of Auditors said. That is why we need to talk about what we can improve in order to achieve this objective in the future, and there are recommendations in the audit report of the Court of Auditors, which we think we need to discuss very well. The Court of Auditors stressed in its previous examination that indirect support should be included; in other words, all tax savings or tax reimbursements should be recorded in order to better assess their effectiveness from a tax-related point of view. This will create a better overall overview, i.e. the total funding that a company or organisation will receive. This is also really set out and transparent, and thus the database can also be managed transparently. The Court of Auditors also complained that there was no declaration of completeness on the part of the Ministries. Moreover, while there is a certain commitment in the field of the countries to cover the supply of services, there is not the disbursements. A few countries volunteer all payments. This voluntaryity is exactly the point: It does not help if the data are only entered voluntarily. Without complete registration of payments both by the ministries and the countries, it will never come to a complete overview, that is simply fact. However, we need this complete overview in order for the database to fulfill its purpose. We believe that these recommendations by the Court of Auditors should be discussed so that the transparency database really deserves its name. Thank you.

At least five have said that they have a certain benefit by using the transparency database, especially when it comes to multiple promotions or maximum limits. Two bodies have indicated that they do not benefit because the data in the transparency database are not relevant for the award of their services. ParlaMint-AT_2021-09-22-027-XXVII-NRSITZ-00121_d7e32649	Mr President. Dear ladies and gentlemen! I would also like to start by thanking you once again, namely for expressing your appreciation for the role of the Court of Auditors and, above all, for the chairman and for all the members of the Court of Auditors. As you can see, twelve reports are on the agenda today; the Court of Auditors is constantly trying to ensure transparency and control in all areas and to present them here. These reports are intended to provide you with a basis for parliamentary work and a contribution to its improvement. This concerns all areas of the State and this also concerns the area of covid support, which has now been mentioned here. However, I do not want to comment on this report because it is a report we are discussing on 8 October. It was sent on the basis of a statement of opinion and published in advance. In my opinion, it is important that we all learn lessons from the crisis together and, I think, all of Austria, and that is also necessary. To this end, the Court of Auditors is also here: to say what is going on. There are twelve reports on the agenda today, and I would like to thank all the previous speakers, as has already been said. However, I would also like to thank honourable Members Schmidhofer for the constructive cooperation and for supporting the work of the Court of Auditors, since he is now leaving the Court of Auditors. [[I should like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report.]] I am brief on the report on the Bundesfinanzgericht and on the report on the database of transparency, because these two reports were published on 29 March. The meeting was held on 15 June with the Minister for Finance. The Federal Finance Court, as you all know, replaced the independent financial Senate in 2014 and should allow for effective legal protection with a reasonable period of procedure in all matters of public charges and financial criminal law. However, this goal could not be achieved for several reasons, 28 percent of legal proceedings lasted more than three years, 17 percent even more than four years. There were large file residues, and we could not see a positive trend towards the reduction of residues during the examination. The duration of the proceedings had even extended compared to the independent financial Senate. As a result, what has already been said by Mr Fuchs, of course, is that tax revenue cannot be achieved because the collection is suspended. However, more than one third of the submissions were rejected by the financial court, thus confirming the content of the decision of the financial and customs administration. Regrettably, withdrawals under referrals also took a very long time, although it is actually necessary to decide relatively quickly on the matter in order to ensure adequate legal protection. The fact is that, unlike the AVG, the formal conclusion of the investigation procedure does not occur in the BAO. This is a legal question which the National Council has to ask itself if one wants to have it, because it also opposes the acceleration of the proceedings. The Court also saw shortcomings in the electronic process for the exchange and transfer of documents to the Bundesfinanzgericht in judicial proceedings. This was laborious and there were problems with the transfer of documents to the so-called BFG interface. There was also no uniform approach to documentation. One problem was the decrease in judicial staff in the test period. If one compares the situation with that of the Federal Administrative Court, one recognizes a significant difference between administrative staff and judicial staff, so that judges and judges had to carry out many administrative activities. However, I also want to acknowledge that, as the Court of Auditors has done, the Ministry of Finance and the Financial Court are now working intensively to address the problems. The objective is to ensure that the objectives of the Bundesfinanzgericht's establishment are actually achieved in the future. I look forward to this with interest. As far as the report on the transparency database is concerned, it is already a follow-up review; we have seen if there was a further development, because the first report comes from 2017. We published this report in spring. The objective of the transparency database is twofold, and that is why it is so difficult: On the one hand, public services are presented in terms of services and, on the other, information on the more efficient use of public funds, namely the important areas of control and control. There are still use deficits, and of course there is still a lack of basics. The problem is – and that has still not been solved –: In order for the whole to function, it takes a coordinated overall approach to the transparency database, across all local authorities. However, there have been improvements, payments of around EUR 486 billion have already been recorded in the transparency database. The costs for the development of the database itself amounted to EUR 15 million over the ten years, so it is very important that the transparency database is used and usable and you ask: Does she keep what she promises? – she promises a lot – and: Has the implementation of a number of recommendations improved usability? 13 recommendations have been implemented, five partially, four not. For example, funding to local authorities and local authorities has been included in the transparency database, and performance commitments have now been made. Ministries would have the opportunity to evaluate the data for management purposes in anonymized form and also to award orders to Statistics Austria, which has so far only been done by the Ministry of Finance. We see a real lack in relation to the completeness of the transparency database, as there were still reports in the area of rehabilitation funds, family allowances, child care benefits. The Ministry of Finance did not require a declaration of completeness, and there is still no legal guarantee of the transparency database. In other words, the federal government must report service offers and payments and thus report more than countries and municipalities. Countries are not obliged to register payments and municipalities are not required to register service offers or payments. Many countries have just voluntarily registered payments. There were big differences. The country of Upper Austria is positive, from which 50 percent of the entries come. Without full entry of payments, however, the essential purpose of the transparency database to have a cross-border overview of all public services is not realized, and thus the benefits are limited. There was an expert round in 2018, which was concerned about whether and how to get a coordinated overall concept. That's not what's changed. The questions are as follows: Which services should be included in the transparency database for what purpose and how can the data be used accordingly? This is always a topic: Data quality and data usage. To sum up, I would like to stress: Ununity reporting obligations of the federal government, countries and municipalities as well as messages lead to a limited use of the transparency database, in particular for cross-border control and control, but it has the potential – and it has already been invested in it – to be further developed. For the most part, there is the possibility to improve control processes and also to provide relevant information, such as the distribution effect of public services. – Thank you for the attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2021-09-22-027-XXVII-NRSITZ-00121_d7e32989	MMag is speaking. DDr. Hubert Fuchs. - Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_2021-09-22-027-XXVII-NRSITZ-00121_d7e33014	Mr President. Madam President of the Court of Auditors! I refer to item 11 on the Court of Auditors' report on international exchange of information on tax matters. International exchange of information on tax matters is becoming increasingly important in view of increasing mobility and globalisation. National tax authorities are increasingly at borders because their investigation possibilities end at the national border. However, the analysis of the Court of Auditors' report gives the impression that a timely and effective EU-wide exchange of information in tax matters is not really wanted. According to the Court of Auditors report, successful EU-wide co-operation was opposed to a lack of cooperation between foreign financial authorities, the pursuit of national interests of some EU Member States, and thus a long period of procedure. However, aggressive tax avoidance models need to be fought together and permanently across the EU, because they distort competition and, of course, lead to a reduction in tax revenue in the EU. In particular, seven EU Member States, with their tax laws, make it possible to: aggressive tax planning. I would like to mention this briefly: Belgium, Ireland, Luxembourg, Malta, the Netherlands, Hungary and Cyprus. According to the Court of Auditors' report, the large-scale audit pointed to the lack of cooperation between other foreign financial authorities, particularly Switzerland and Ireland, in their participation in official assistance and the problem of so-called bartering strategies. This includes long waiting times as well as precise and repeated demand. I should like to stress once again: This is not about requests for assistance to any Caribbean State, but about requests for assistance within the European Union or Switzerland. A case of large-scale audit is documented by the Court of Auditors as requested cooperation with Ireland took longer than one year and concrete results could not be achieved after this year. According to the large-scale audit, the procedure could take years to exploit all opposition possibilities if the Irish financial authority would not at all reject the entire request for assistance. Some countries – including Switzerland and Ireland – even need the consent of the taxable person for the transmission of information. – It is likely that a taxable person will rarely agree that his tax affairs will be transferred to a foreign tax authority. Let me briefly address the unsatisfying response times of the foreign financial authorities to requests for assistance: On average, Austria does more than 70 percent of individual requests for assistance within 90 days, which is a very good value. Conversely, however, Austria has to wait a very long time for replies from abroad: Only 29 percent of the replies to direct taxes came within 90 days – we meet 70 percent – and about indirect taxes were 52 percent. Switzerland, repeatedly mentioned, and Turkey needs more than one year to respond in more than half of the cases. Other countries with a response duration of more than a year were Luxembourg, Cyprus and Japan. Such long response times are not conducive to correct and timely clarification of tax behaviour. The Minister of Finance is obliged to point out in Brussels on a permanent basis the fiscal importance of the international exchange of information on tax matters and their rapid implementation. It is not enough if this is only done at official level, and the Minister of Finance needs to be involved in this in Brussels, and that is not really pleasant. The problem states are known, which belongs permanently to the EU level. Only with a properly functioning international exchange of information can tax fraud be effectively combated, but apparently this is not really desirable within the European Union. Thank you.
